I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I guess what brought me in is, at work I’ve been making these kind of stupid mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I guess what brought me in is, at work I’ve been making these kind of stupid mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah… thanks for seeing me. I guess what brought me in is, at work I’ve been making these kind of stupid mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your distractions or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t think it’s like I’m trying to upset anybody, but at home it can turn into arguments because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I forget or get sidetracked, and they feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nAnd… I guess it’s not just home. At work it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was embarrassing. So, yeah, it does affect things, even if I try not to let it. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I don’t remember anyone giving it a name or anything like that when I was a kid, and I wasn’t in treatment for it.\n\nI think it was more just… teachers correcting me, my mom getting on me about school stuff, that kind of thing. But no formal diagnosis or medication or counseling that I can remember. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or anything like that… at least not so far. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or succeed in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, unfortunately. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah… thanks for seeing me. I guess what brought me in is, at work I’ve been making these kind of stupid mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your distractions or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t think it’s like I’m trying to upset anybody, but at home it can turn into arguments because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I forget or get sidetracked, and they feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nAnd… I guess it’s not just home. At work it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was embarrassing. So, yeah, it does affect things, even if I try not to let it. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I don’t remember anyone giving it a name or anything like that when I was a kid, and I wasn’t in treatment for it.\n\nI think it was more just… teachers correcting me, my mom getting on me about school stuff, that kind of thing. But no formal diagnosis or medication or counseling that I can remember. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or anything like that… at least not so far. But it has caused some real problems.\n\nAt work, it got serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was pretty embarrassing. And at home, it definitely adds to conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe not the worst-case consequences, but it’s not nothing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or succeed in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but at work it got to the point where my manager actually put it in a formal review, so that definitely felt like it was affecting my career.\n\nAnd at home, it causes conflict too, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. So… yeah, I guess it has gotten in the way of me doing as well as I’d like. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your social relationships, such as friendships, due to your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially, I guess it shows up more at home than with friends, if that makes sense. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say it’s been equally consistent my whole adult life. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your distractions or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t think it’s like I’m trying to upset anybody, but at home it can turn into arguments because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I forget or get sidetracked, and they feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nAnd… I guess it’s not just home. At work it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was embarrassing. So, yeah, it does affect things, even if I try not to let it. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I don’t remember anyone giving it a name or anything like that when I was a kid, and I wasn’t in treatment for it.\n\nI think it was more just… teachers correcting me, my mom getting on me about school stuff, that kind of thing. But no formal diagnosis or medication or counseling that I can remember. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or anything like that… at least not so far. But it has caused some real problems.\n\nAt work, it got serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was pretty embarrassing. And at home, it definitely adds to conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe not the worst-case consequences, but it’s not nothing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or succeed in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but at work it got to the point where my manager actually put it in a formal review, so that definitely felt like it was affecting my career.\n\nAnd at home, it causes conflict too, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. So… yeah, I guess it has gotten in the way of me doing as well as I’d like. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your social relationships, such as friendships, due to your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially, I guess it shows up more at home than with friends, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’ve had friendships end over it or anything like that.\n\nBut at home it does cause conflict, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. And at work it’s been serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, so… I guess it does affect relationships in that way too.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but it’s definitely been an issue. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say it’s been equally consistent my whole adult life. I mean, I’ve probably had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago.\n\nThat’s when it started feeling harder to manage and like it was actually affecting things. I don’t know if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with distraction and restlessness for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but it’s been a real issue since I moved into the supervisor role, which was about a year and two months ago now.\n\nSo yes, definitely more than six months. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do sometimes. I try not to, because I know it comes off rude, but in meetings especially I’ll jump in before someone’s finished because I think I already know where they’re going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I kind of cut them off and they weren’t done. It’s not like I’m trying to talk over people, but… yeah, it does happen. Is that what you mean by interrupting?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your distractions or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t think it’s like I’m trying to upset anybody, but at home it can turn into arguments because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I forget or get sidetracked, and they feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nAnd… I guess it’s not just home. At work it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was embarrassing. So, yeah, it does affect things, even if I try not to let it. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I don’t remember anyone giving it a name or anything like that when I was a kid, and I wasn’t in treatment for it.\n\nI think it was more just… teachers correcting me, my mom getting on me about school stuff, that kind of thing. But no formal diagnosis or medication or counseling that I can remember. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or anything like that… at least not so far. But it has caused some real problems.\n\nAt work, it got serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was pretty embarrassing. And at home, it definitely adds to conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe not the worst-case consequences, but it’s not nothing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or succeed in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but at work it got to the point where my manager actually put it in a formal review, so that definitely felt like it was affecting my career.\n\nAnd at home, it causes conflict too, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. So… yeah, I guess it has gotten in the way of me doing as well as I’d like. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your social relationships, such as friendships, due to your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially, I guess it shows up more at home than with friends, if that makes sense. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say it’s been equally consistent my whole adult life. I mean, I’ve probably had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago.\n\nThat’s when it started feeling harder to manage and like it was actually affecting things. I don’t know if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with distraction and restlessness for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do sometimes. I try not to, because I know it comes off rude, but in meetings especially I’ll jump in before someone’s finished because I think I already know where they’re going.\n\nAnd then sometimes I realize I answered the wrong thing, or I kind of cut them off and they weren’t done. It’s not like I’m trying to talk over people, but… yeah, it does happen. Is that what you mean by interrupting?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been unable to perform a task or achieve a goal due to your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m completely unable, exactly, but it has gotten to where it’s affected things.\n\nAt work, my manager actually put it in a formal review, which was… embarrassing. It was basically about me not staying on top of tasks the way I’m supposed to. And at home, it causes conflict too, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nSo, yes, I guess it has gotten in the way of doing what I’m supposed to do. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever mention that you had significant difficulties with attention or impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I didn’t think of it as a big deal back then, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, that kind of thing. I mean, I didn’t think of it as a big deal back then, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, that kind of thing. And apparently I had trouble staying in my seat unless someone reminded me.\n\nI’m not trying to make it sound worse than it was, but… yeah, it sounds like it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking about?\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 36 / first_line_supervisor_of_non_retail_sales_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Blurting_Answers\nmanifestation: \"In meetings I jump in before people finish talking because I think I know where they're going, and then I realize I answered the wrong point or cut somebody off.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Pleasing patient.\nA pleasing patient may\n1) minimize, downplay, or describe in more favorable terms symptoms and behaviors that they believe could create a negative impression,\n2) initially withhold or delay disclosing sensitive clinical information due to shame or fear of being judged, and\n3) seek confirmation that their descriptions or answers are appropriate and demonstrate a tendency to seek the doctor's approval.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… not in a reckless way, I don’t think. But yes, sometimes I speak before I really think it through.

Like in meetings, I’ll jump in because I assume I know what someone is about to say, and then I realize I answered the wrong point or I cut them off before they finished. It’s embarrassing, because I’m not trying to be disrespectful. It just comes out too fast sometimes. Is that what you mean by acting on impulse?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah… thanks for seeing me. I guess what brought me in is, at work I’ve been making these kind of stupid mistakes that I really shouldn’t be making. I supervise a sales team, and I’ll be doing reports and somehow copy the wrong numbers from the team dashboard, or I’ll miss a line in a procedure I was supposed to check.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be careless—I’ll even think I went back over it—but then someone catches it later. It’s embarrassing, honestly. I don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you’d want to hear about, but it’s been happening enough that I figured I should ask someone.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself easily distracted while working on these reports and procedures?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I’m just not trying, because I really do try to focus. But yes, I do get pulled off track pretty easily.\n\nLike if I’m reviewing call notes or checking numbers, sometimes just one conversation across the room catches my attention, or I’ll suddenly think of something I need to handle at home, and then I’m off track before I even realize it. Is that the kind of distraction you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah… somewhat. I mean, I can sit still if I have to, like in meetings, but it’s not exactly comfortable.\n\nIn longer meetings especially, it kind of feels like my body is buzzing on the inside, if that makes sense. I’ll shift around a lot, tap my foot under the table, or sometimes I’ll look for a reason to step out for a minute. I try not to make it obvious or bother anyone, but… yeah, it does happen.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or overwhelmed lately?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure I’d call it anxious, exactly. Maybe overwhelmed sometimes, but I figure most people feel that way with work and everything.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it. It’s more like I get frustrated when I’m trying to keep up or when I realize I missed something, and then I start worrying a bit about how it looks. But I don’t know if that’s the same as anxiety. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or get easily distracted when you're not working, like when you're reading or watching TV?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least some. It’s probably not as noticeable as at work, or maybe I just don’t keep track of it the same way.\n\nBut even with something simple, like if I’m trying to read something or follow a show, my mind can jump to some random thing I need to do at home, or I’ll catch part of a conversation nearby and realize I missed what I was doing. It’s kind of like I’m off track before I even notice it. Does that count?\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a major way. I mean, I have off days like anyone, where I’m tired or kind of frustrated, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling really sad or down lately.\n\nAt least, not that I’ve noticed. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing any feelings of guilt or worthlessness lately?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, no. I mean, I feel bad if I mess something up or if I feel like I let someone down, but I don’t think I’d call it worthlessness.\n\nGuilt… maybe in the normal way, I guess? Like wishing I’d handled something better. But I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m a bad person or anything. Is that kind of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these distractions and restlessness at home or when you're alone, or is it mainly at work?\n\nPatient: It’s not only at work, no. I mean, work makes it more obvious because there’s more going on, but it can happen at home too.\n\nLike when I’m trying to review call notes or numbers, one conversation across the room—or even just some random thought about something I need to do at home—can pull me off track before I realize I stopped paying attention. So… yeah, I guess it’s not just a work thing. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you always been this easily distracted and restless, even as a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound worse than it was, but my mom still brings it up sometimes.\n\nShe says teachers would send notes home in elementary school about me rushing through my work, losing homework, talking when I wasn’t supposed to, and not staying in my seat unless someone reminded me. So… yeah, I guess it’s been there since I was a kid. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your distractions or restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, a little. I don’t think it’s like I’m trying to upset anybody, but at home it can turn into arguments because I’ll say I’m going to do something and then I forget or get sidetracked, and they feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through.\n\nAnd… I guess it’s not just home. At work it’s gotten serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was embarrassing. So, yeah, it does affect things, even if I try not to let it. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these symptoms when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I mean, I don’t remember anyone giving it a name or anything like that when I was a kid, and I wasn’t in treatment for it.\n\nI think it was more just… teachers correcting me, my mom getting on me about school stuff, that kind of thing. But no formal diagnosis or medication or counseling that I can remember. Does that help?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever lost a job, had a relationship end, or experienced other significant consequences because of your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say I’ve outright lost a job or anything like that… at least not so far. But it has caused some real problems.\n\nAt work, it got serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, which was pretty embarrassing. And at home, it definitely adds to conflict because people feel like they can’t count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something.\n\nSo… yeah, maybe not the worst-case consequences, but it’s not nothing. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with distraction and restlessness significantly impacted your ability to achieve your goals or succeed in your career?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it has, unfortunately. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but at work it got to the point where my manager actually put it in a formal review, so that definitely felt like it was affecting my career.\n\nAnd at home, it causes conflict too, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. So… yeah, I guess it has gotten in the way of me doing as well as I’d like. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any difficulties in your social relationships, such as friendships, due to your difficulties with distraction and restlessness?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… socially, I guess it shows up more at home than with friends, if that makes sense. I don’t think I’ve had friendships end over it or anything like that.\n\nBut at home it does cause conflict, because people feel like they can’t really count on me to follow through when I say I’ll do something. And at work it’s been serious enough that my manager put it in a formal review, so… I guess it does affect relationships in that way too.\n\nI don’t want to overstate it, but it’s definitely been an issue. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with distraction and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I’m not sure I’d say it’s been equally consistent my whole adult life. I mean, I’ve probably had some tendencies before, but it became a real problem when I moved into the supervisor role, which was about… a year and two months ago.\n\nThat’s when it started feeling harder to manage and like it was actually affecting things. I don’t know if that answers it the right way.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with distraction and restlessness for at least 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but it’s been a real issue since I moved into the supervisor role, which was about a year and two months ago now.\n\nSo yes, definitely more than six months. Is that the kind of timeframe you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they are speaking?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do sometimes. It’s not like I’m trying to talk over people, but… yeah, it does happen. It was basically about me not staying on top of tasks the way I’m supposed to.     "functional_impairment": true,
```json
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "onset of symptoms prior to age 12 confirmed",
      "symptoms have caused significant impairment in social and occupational functioning"
    ]
  }
}
```
